\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic} proves
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}.  Nonnegativity of relative
entropy then gives \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Raw-tail Poisson-energy decomposition]
\label{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a,
 \qquad
 \Phi(v)=(1+v)\log(1+v)-v.
\]
For \(t>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential}
 V_t^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>t\}}F_y(x/t)\,\nu_c(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the covariance-proxy defect in
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 =t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(1),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation or absolute-continuity hypothesis is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation permits us to assume that \(\nu\) is centred.  Set
\[
 \delta_t(y)=\int\{F_y(x/t)-1\}\nu(\dd x),
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma,
 \qquad \tau_t=\nu(|x|>t).
\]
Finite second moment gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness}
 \tau_t\le t^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Let \(\ell_y(z)\) and \(B_2(y,z)\) be the homogeneous degree-one and
degree-two Taylor terms of \(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Direct differentiation of
the rational function \(F_y\), together with translation continuity of
the first two derivatives of \(P_1^{(d)}\) in \(L^1(\RR^d)\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}
 \le \varepsilon_d(|z|)|z|^2
\end{equation}
for \(|z|\le1\), where \(\varepsilon_d\) is bounded on \([0,1]\) and
\(\varepsilon_d(r)\to0\) as \(r\downarrow0\).  The Taylor coefficients
themselves satisfy, for \(j=1,2\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms}
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_\infty+
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\le C_d|z|^j,
 \qquad B_1=\ell.
\end{equation}

Centering and the covariance-mode identity
\(\int B_2(y,x)\nu(\dd x)=b_\Sigma(y)\) now yield the exact split
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split}
 \delta_t=u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t+R_t,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 R_t=
 \int_{|x|\le t}\{F_\cdot(x/t)-1-\ell_\cdot(x/t)
                   -B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x)
 -\int_{|x|>t}\{\ell_\cdot(x/t)+B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x).
\]
Equations \eqref{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}
 \|R_t\|_\infty+\|R_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, after multiplication by \(t^2\), the interior remainder is
dominated by \(C_d|x|^2\) and converges pointwise to zero.  The omitted
linear and quadratic tail terms are bounded respectively by
\[
 Ct^{-1}\int_{|x|>t}|x|\nu(\dd x)
 \le Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x).
\]

The identity
\(F_y(z)\Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization}
 V_t^\nu\ge0,
 \qquad \int V_t^\nu\dd\Omega_d=\tau_t.
\end{equation}
We next justify the nonlinear separation.  If \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(v\ge0\),
two integrations of \(\Phi''(s)=(1+s)^{-1}\) over the rectangle joining
\((0,0)\) to \((u,v)\) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-phi-interaction}
 |\Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|\le C|u|v.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}, all negative arguments in
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split} are uniformly larger than \(-1/2\) for
large \(t\).  The mean-value theorem, the bound
\(\log(1+v)\le v\), and \eqref{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization} then give
\begin{align}
 \int|\Phi(\delta_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-mass}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)-\Phi(u_t)-
 \Phi(V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}).
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction}
\end{align}
For clarity, the first left side is bounded by
\(C\|R_t\|_\infty\tau_t+C t^{-2}\|R_t\|_1
 +C\|R_t\|_\infty\|R_t\|_1\); the second is bounded by
\(C\tau_t(\tau_t+t^{-2})\); and the third is at most
\(C\|u_t\|_\infty\tau_t\).  These bounds are all \(o(t^{-4})\).

Finally, boundedness and zero mean of \(b_\Sigma\) imply
\[
 \int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}+O(t^{-6}).
\]
Since \(H_d(t)=\int\Phi(\delta_t)\dd\Omega_d\), equations
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}--
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction} prove
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}.  Comparing it with
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition} proves
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition} and the equivalence.
\end{proof}

For a formulation involving only masses of moving sets, define
\[
 A_{t,0}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ |x-ty|<t\},
\]
and, for \(k\ge1\),
\[
 A_{t,k}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ 2^{k-1}t\le|x-ty|<2^kt\}.
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-functional}
 M_t^\nu(y)=(1+|y|^2)^a\left\{
 \nu_c(A_{t,0}(y))+
 \sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k(d+1)}\nu_c(A_{t,k}(y))\right\}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-energy}
 G_\nu(t)=t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(M_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Moving-annulus raw-tail criterion]
\label{cor:moving-annulus-tail-criterion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition},
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G_\nu(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]

\section{The Covariance-Matched Poisson Defect}
\label{sec:covariance-proxy-defect}

Finite covariance determines the quadratic mode but, in dimensions at least
four, does not force the matched entropy to attain the corresponding
coefficient.  The following result identifies the complete nonnegative
remainder without imposing a tail class or an absolute moment above order
two.

\begin{theorem}[Covariance-proxy defect decomposition]
\label{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
\label{thm:finite-covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with mean
\(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,
 \qquad g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma),
 \qquad H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\]
and let \(b_\Sigma\) be the bounded covariance mode in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}.  For all sufficiently
large \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy}
 k_{\Sigma,t}(x)
 :=g_t(x)\left[1+t^{-2}b_\Sigma
 \left(\frac{x-\bar\gamma}{t}\right)\right]
\end{equation}
is a probability density.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 :=t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
 t^4H_d(t)
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+\mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No density of \(\nu\), regular-variation hypothesis, angular balance, or
higher absolute moment is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After translation, assume \(\bar\gamma=0\), and write
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^{(d+1)/2}.
\]
In normalized coordinates,
\[
 q_t(y):=\frac{h_t(ty)}{g_t(ty)}
 =\EE F_y(X/t),
 \qquad \delta_t=q_t-1,
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma.
\]
The covariance calculation in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} gives
\(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\).  Since \(b_\Sigma\) is bounded,
\(1+u_t\ge1/2\) for all sufficiently large \(t\), proving that
\eqref{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy} is positive and has integral one.

We first establish the only asymptotic input needed below:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}
 t^2\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)\delta_t(y)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 \longrightarrow
 \int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y).
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 J(z)=\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)F_y(z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y.
\]
The function \(b_\Sigma\) is even, smooth, and has bounded second
derivatives.  Translation in the last integral shows that \(J\) is even,
\(J(0)=0\), and its Hessian is bounded on the unit ball.  Hence
\(|J(z)|\le C|z|^2\) for \(|z|\le1\).  For \(|z|\ge1\), positivity and
normalization of the translated Poisson density give
\(|J(z)|\le\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty\le
\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty|z|^2\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing}
 |J(z)|\le C_{d,\Sigma}|z|^2,
 \qquad z\in\RR^d.
\end{equation}

Let \(B_2(y,z)\) denote the homogeneous degree-two Taylor term of
\(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Taylor expansion under the integral gives
\[
 J(z)=\int b_\Sigma(y)B_2(y,z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)+o(|z|^2).
\]
The covariance-mode identity proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} is
\(\EE B_2(y,X)=b_\Sigma(y)\).  Since \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\),
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing} and dominated
convergence imply
\[
 \EE[t^2J(X/t)]
 \longrightarrow
 \int b_\Sigma(y)\EE B_2(y,X)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d.
\]
Because \(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\), the left side is precisely the
left side of \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}.

We also have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness}
 \|\delta_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, Minkowski's integral inequality bounds this norm by
\[
 \EE\left\|P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-X/t)-P_1^{(d)}
 \right\|_{L^1(\RR^d)},
\]
which tends to zero by translation continuity and dominated convergence.

Put \(r_t=\delta_t-u_t\).  Equations
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing}
 t^2\int b_\Sigma r_t\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \|r_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Uniformly in \(y\),
\[
 \log(1+u_t)=u_t+O(t^{-4}).
\]
It follows from \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder}
 \int r_t\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d=o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
On the other hand, with
\(\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\), boundedness of \(b_\Sigma\) gives
\begin{align}
 \int(1+u_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 &=\int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d \notag\\
 &=\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+O(t^{-6}).
 \label{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic}
 \int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}

Finally, the exact logarithmic identity is
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t})
 +\int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d.
\]
 All terms are finite for large \(t\).  Indeed, convexity of relative entropy
 and
 \[
  D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-z)\,\middle\|\,
  P_1^{(d)}\right)
  \le \frac{d+1}{2}\log\{2(1+|z|^2)\}
  \le C_d(1+|z|^2)
 \]
 bound \(H_d(t)\) by an integrable average under
 \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\).  The first inequality follows from
 \(1+|w+z|^2\le2(1+|w|^2)(1+|z|^2)\).  Moreover,
 \(\log(1+u_t)\) is bounded.  Substitution of
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic} proves
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}.  Nonnegativity of relative
entropy then gives \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define

\subsection{Imported symbol criterion and the Poisson integer-orbit symbol}%
\label{subsec:lattice-background-poisson-symbol}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The classical principal-symbol sampling criterion, with its hypotheses and
proof, is recorded in \path{sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex}, paragraph
``Imported shift-invariant-space criterion,'' as compiled by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\phantomsection\label{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}
\else
\paragraph{Imported shift-invariant-space criterion.}
This paragraph is the only abstract sampling input used in the integer-orbit
argument.  The background lemma records the classical principal
shift-invariant-space/Riesz-translate criterion under the hypotheses displayed
in its statement: a Toeplitz Gram matrix with $a\in\ell^1(\ZZ)$, a principal
symbol bounded above and below away from zero, and a restriction map whose
Riesz-coordinate action is the Gram operator
\cite{deBoorDeVoreRon1994,RonShen1995,Bownik2000,AldroubiGrochenig2001}; it is
compatible with the Beurling--Landau density tradition and its RKHS sampling
descendants
\cite{Beurling1989,Landau1967,Seip1992,NashedWalter1991,NashedSun2010,ChengJiangSun2016,FuhrGrochenigHaimiKlotzRomero2018}.
The Poisson-specific
inputs are the closed strip-kernel symbol in
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} and the observation-channel
identification in Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}.  The Riesz bounds,
cardinal Fourier formula and norm, and projection/quotient theorem then follow
as closed-form consequences of these inputs and the imported criterion; related
integer-shift and nod-function computations appear in
\cite{KiselevMininNovikov2016,KiselevMininNovikov2019}.

\smallskip
\begin{lemma}[Classical imported principal-symbol sampling criterion]%
\label{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}
This is a background result in the principal shift-invariant-space and
RKHS sampling literature, used here in the form of
\cite{deBoorDeVoreRon1994,RonShen1995,Bownik2000,AldroubiGrochenig2001,NashedWalter1991,NashedSun2010,ChengJiangSun2016}.
The density theorems
\cite{Beurling1989,Landau1967,Seip1992,FuhrGrochenigHaimiKlotzRomero2018}
motivate the terminology and comparison class; the proof below uses only the
displayed positive-symbol hypothesis.
Let $\mathcal H$ be a Hilbert space and let
$(\varphi_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$ be a family whose Gram matrix is Toeplitz,
\[
\langle\varphi_m,\varphi_n\rangle_{\mathcal H}=a_{m-n},
\qquad a\in\ell^1(\ZZ).
\]
Put
\[
\sigma(\theta):=\sum_{k\in\ZZ}a_ke^{-\ii k\theta},
\qquad -\pi\le\theta\le\pi,
\]
and suppose that $0<A\le\sigma(\theta)\le B<\infty$ a.e.  Then the
synthesis map
\[
C:c_{00}(\ZZ)\to\mathcal H,
\qquad Cc:=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_n\varphi_n,
\]
extends to a topological isomorphism from $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ onto the closed
orbit space $V:=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{\varphi_n:n\in\ZZ\}$, with
\[
A\|c\|_{\ell^2}^2\le\|Cc\|_{\mathcal H}^2
\le B\|c\|_{\ell^2}^2.
\]
Let $G$ be the Toeplitz Gram operator on $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ with matrix
$(a_{m-n})_{m,n\in\ZZ}$.  The discrete Fourier transform diagonalizes
$G$ as multiplication by~$\sigma$, and $G^{-1}$ is multiplication by
$\sigma^{-1}$.  If, in addition, a bounded restriction map
$R:V\to\ell^2(\ZZ)$ satisfies $R(Cc)=Gc$ for all finitely supported $c$,
then $R$ is an isomorphism.  For every datum $\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ the
unique interpolant $R^{-1}\alpha$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:imported-reciprocal-symbol-norm}
\|R^{-1}\alpha\|_{\mathcal H}^2
=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta .
\end{equation}
The cardinal element $L:=R^{-1}\delta_0$ is synthesized by
$d=G^{-1}\delta_0$, equivalently $\widetilde d=\sigma^{-1}$ a.e.
Thus the statement supplies the functional-analytic passage from a positive
principal symbol to Riesz bounds, bounded interpolation, and the
reciprocal-symbol norm.  In the Poisson setting,
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs} verify these displayed hypotheses for
the strip kernel and observation profiles
\cite{deBoorDeVoreRon1994,RonShen1995,AldroubiGrochenig2001}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The standard Fourier proof in the notation of this paper is recalled in
Appendix~\ref{app:background-symbol-criterion}.
\end{proof}

Appendix~\ref{app:background-symbol-criterion} recalls the standard Fourier
proof in the present notation.  Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}
is used only as imported background; below, the article-specific input is the
computation of the particular Poisson-strip symbol \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}
and its use for the observation-channel projection.
\fi

\paragraph{Poisson-specific integer-orbit symbol.}
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling} becomes an
article-specific statement only after
the Poisson strip kernel is inserted.  The next proposition is the calculation
that foregrounds the integer-orbit contribution used by the observation-channel
projection theorem below.

\begin{proposition}[Explicit Poisson integer-orbit symbol]%
\label{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}
Let
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2},
\qquad
\sigma(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}K(n,0)e^{-\ii n\theta}
=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\frac{2}{4+n^2}e^{-\ii n\theta}.
\]
Then $\sigma$ is continuous and $2\pi$-periodic, and for
$\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-symbol}
\sigma(\theta)
=\frac{\pi\cosh(2(\pi-|\theta|))}{\sinh(2\pi)}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the sharp lattice-symbol bounds are
\[
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)}
\le
\sigma(\theta)
\le
\pi\coth(2\pi)=:B_{\ZZ}
\qquad(\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]).
\]
Moreover, for every finitely supported scalar sequence
$c=(c_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$, with
\[
\widetilde c(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_ne^{-\ii n\theta},
\]
one has the exact Gram identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-symbol-form}
\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,K(m,n)
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\sigma(\theta)|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The periodized-exponential calculation proving this proposition is in
\path{sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex}, proposition ``Explicit Poisson
integer-orbit symbol,'' as compiled by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
\begin{proof}
Set $k(x):=K(x,0)=2/(4+x^2)$.  With the Fourier convention used in this
paper,
\[
\widehat k(\xi)=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}.
\]
The defining series for $\sigma$ is absolutely and uniformly convergent,
so $\sigma$ is continuous and $2\pi$-periodic.
The Poisson summation formula \cite[Chapter~III]{Stein1971} gives
\[
\sigma(\theta)=\sum_{m\in\ZZ}\widehat k(\theta+2\pi m)
=\pi\sum_{m\in\ZZ}e^{-2|\theta+2\pi m|}.
\]
For $0\le\theta\le\pi$ this geometric series equals
\[
\pi\left(e^{-2\theta}
+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-2(\theta+2\pi j)}
+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-2(2\pi j-\theta)}\right)
=\pi\,\frac{\cosh(2(\pi-\theta))}{\sinh(2\pi)}.
\]
The expression is even in~$\theta$, so \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol} follows
on $[-\pi,\pi]$, and periodicity gives the full symbol.
The minimum and maximum of the displayed formula occur respectively at
$|\theta|=\pi$ and $\theta=0$, which gives the sharp constants
$A_{\ZZ}$ and $B_{\ZZ}$.

Finally, since $K(m,n)=k(m-n)$, Parseval's identity for the discrete
Fourier transform gives
\[
\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,K(m,n)
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\left(\sum_{\ell\in\ZZ}k(\ell)e^{-\ii\ell\theta}\right)
|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta,
\]
which is exactly \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol-form}.
\end{proof}
\fi

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
Define
\[
\mathcal H_K(\ZZ):=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}
\subset\mathcal H_K,
\qquad
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)},\quad
B_{\ZZ}:=\pi\coth(2\pi).
\]
The Riesz bounds, restriction isomorphism, sampling inequality, and their
proof are in \path{sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex}, paragraph
``Consequences of the imported criterion for the Poisson symbol,'' collected
by \path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
\paragraph{Consequences of the imported criterion for the Poisson symbol.}
Define the closed shift-generated Hilbert subspaces
\[
\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)
:=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}
\subset \mathcal H_K,
\]
\[
\mathcal S_{\ZZ}
:=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{\Psi_n:n\in\ZZ\}
\subset L^2_0(\omega).
\]
Thus $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and $\mathcal S_{\ZZ}$ are the Hilbert closures
of finite linear combinations of the corresponding kernel sections and
secants.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-sampling-constants}
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)},
\qquad
B_{\ZZ}:=\pi\coth(2\pi).
\end{equation}
We apply the imported principal-symbol criterion
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling} once, with the Toeplitz
kernel and symbol computed in

\subsection{Poisson cardinal projection and observation-channel samples}%
\label{subsec:poisson-cardinal-observation}

After inserting the Poisson symbol of
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} into the imported
principal-symbol criterion, the cardinal kernel is an explicit consequence of
standard interpolation in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.  The Poisson observation theorem
below is this lattice projection applied to secant profiles: the samples
determine the lattice projection and quotient class, and its statement also
identifies the exact law-side condition for full ambient recovery.

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The secondary explicit-cardinal-kernel corollary is in
\path{sec_strip_30_cardinal_observation.tex}, Corollary ``Explicit lattice
cardinal kernel and norm,'' collected by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.  It defines
\(L=R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\delta_0\), proves cardinal interpolation and compact-uniform
convergence, and gives the reciprocal-symbol norm and Fourier formulas labeled
\path{eq:cardinal-norm-formula} and \path{eq:cardinal-kernel-fourier} there.
\else
\begin{corollary}[Explicit lattice cardinal kernel and norm]%
\label{cor:poisson-cardinal-reconstruction}
With the Poisson symbol consequences recorded immediately after
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}, let
\[
L:=R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\delta_0\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ).
\]
Here $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ is the closed span of the kernel sections
$\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}$.
Then $L(n)=\delta_{0n}$ for every $n\in\ZZ$.  For every
$\alpha=(\alpha_n)_{n\in\ZZ}\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$, the series
\[
F_\alpha(z):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_nL(z-n)
\]
converges in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and uniformly on compact subsets of the
strip $\mathbb S$, and it is the unique function in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$
satisfying
\[
F_\alpha(n)=\alpha_n
\qquad(n\in\ZZ).
\]
If
\[
\widetilde\alpha(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_ne^{-\ii n\theta},
\qquad \theta\in[-\pi,\pi],
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cardinal-norm-formula}
\|F_\alpha\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta,
\end{equation}
where $\sigma$ is the lattice symbol from \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}.  If
$d\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ is the unique sequence satisfying
\[
\widetilde d(\theta)=\sigma(\theta)^{-1}
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],
\]
then
\[
L(z)=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}d_nK(z,n)
\]
in $\mathcal H_K$, and for almost every $\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]$ and every
$m\in\ZZ$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cardinal-kernel-fourier}
\widehat L(\theta+2\pi m)
=\frac{\pi e^{-2|\theta+2\pi m|}}{\sigma(\theta)}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $n\in\ZZ$ define the translation operator
$\tau_nF(z):=F(z-n)$.  Since
\[
K(z-n,m)=K(z,m+n),
\]
integer translations preserve $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.  By definition,
$R_{\ZZ}L=\delta_0$.  If $S_n$ denotes the shift operator on
$\ell^2(\ZZ)$ given by $(S_na)_m:=a_{m-n}$, then
$R_{\ZZ}\tau_n=S_nR_{\ZZ}$, so for every $n\in\ZZ$
one has
\[
R_{\ZZ}(\tau_nL)=S_n\delta_0=\delta_n.
\]
Hence $\tau_nL$ is the unique interpolant of $\delta_n$, and therefore
$L(n)=\delta_{0n}$.

If $\alpha$ has finite support, then
\[
R_{\ZZ}\left(\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_nL(\cdot-n)\right)
=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_n\delta_n
=\alpha.
\]
By the uniqueness part of
the standard sampling consequences of
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}, the corresponding
interpolant
is exactly
\[
F_\alpha=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_nL(\cdot-n).
\]
For general $\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$, let $\alpha^{(N)}$ be the truncation
to $\{-N,\dots,N\}$.  Then $\alpha^{(N)}\to\alpha$ in $\ell^2(\ZZ)$, and
the continuity of $R_{\ZZ}^{-1}$ implies
\[
F_{\alpha^{(N)}}=R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\alpha^{(N)}
\longrightarrow
R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\alpha=:F_\alpha
\]
in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.  Since each $F_{\alpha^{(N)}}$ is a finite
cardinal sum, the series for $F_\alpha$ converges in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:gram-space-poisson-image} gives the uniform estimate
\[
|F(z)|\le \frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1-r^2)}}\,\|F\|_{\mathcal H_K}
\qquad(|\Im z|\le r<1),
\]
so $\mathcal H_K$-norm convergence implies uniform convergence on every
compact subset of~$\mathbb S$.  The interpolation identity for $F_\alpha$
follows from the continuity of $R_{\ZZ}$.

The norm formula is the reciprocal-symbol identity
\eqref{eq:imported-reciprocal-symbol-norm} from
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling} with the Poisson symbol
\eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}.  Concretely, first suppose that $\alpha$
has finite support and write
\[
F_\alpha=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_nK(\cdot,n).
\]
Then $Gc=\alpha$, where $G$ is the Toeplitz Gram operator from the proof
of the same imported criterion.  In the discrete Fourier
representation this becomes
\[
\widetilde\alpha(\theta)=\sigma(\theta)\widetilde c(\theta)
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\theta\in[-\pi,\pi].
\]
Using \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol-form}, we obtain
\[
\|F_\alpha\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\sigma(\theta)|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta.
\]
Since $1/\sigma\in L^\infty([-\pi,\pi])$, the right-hand side is
continuous in $\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$, so the formula extends to all
$\alpha$ by density.

Now let $d:=G^{-1}\delta_0$.  Then $R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\delta_0=L$ gives
\[
L=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}d_nK(\cdot,n)
\]
in $\mathcal H_K$, and the discrete Fourier transform diagonalizes $G$,
so
\[
\widetilde d(\theta)=\sigma(\theta)^{-1}
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\theta\in[-\pi,\pi].
\]
Taking Fourier transforms on~$\RR$ and using
$\widehat{K(\cdot,n)}(\xi)=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}e^{-\ii n\xi}$, we find
\[
\widehat L(\xi)
=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}\sum_{n\in\ZZ}d_ne^{-\ii n\xi}.
\]
Writing $\xi=\theta+2\pi m$ with $\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]$ and $m\in\ZZ$,
the periodicity of the discrete Fourier series yields
\[
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}d_ne^{-\ii n(\theta+2\pi m)}
=\widetilde d(\theta)
=\sigma(\theta)^{-1},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:cardinal-kernel-fourier}.
\end{proof}
\fi

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The secondary Fourier-uniqueness lemma showing that the full continuous
profile determines the centred law is in
\path{sec_strip_30_cardinal_observation.tex}, Lemma ``Observation profile
determines the centred law,'' collected by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
\begin{observationprofilelemma}[Observation profile determines the centred law]%
\label{lem:observation-profile-determines-law}
Write \(X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\).  Let \(\kappa_t\) be the distribution of
\(X_c/t\), and define the finite signed
measure
\[
\lambda_t(\dd s):=s\,\kappa_t(\dd s).
\]
For the Poisson observation channel in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling},
\[
q_{F_t}(\xi)=e^{2|\xi|}\widehat F_t(\xi)=\pi\,\widehat{\lambda_t}(\xi)
\]
with the Fourier convention used throughout this section.  Hence \(q_{F_t}\)
determines \(\lambda_t\) by Fourier uniqueness.  It determines \(\kappa_t\) on
\(\RR\setminus\{0\}\) by
\[
\kappa_t(B)=\int_B s^{-1}\lambda_t(\dd s),
\qquad 0\notin\overline B,
\]
and then determines the atom at the origin from total mass:
\[
\kappa_t(\{0\})=1-\kappa_t(\RR\setminus\{0\}).
\]
Thus the full continuous profile \(q_{F_t}\) determines the centred law of
\(X_c/t\).  For the integer-orbit cardinal reconstruction formula to
represent this full channel, \(q_{F_t}\) must be \(2\pi\)-periodic,
equivalently \(\kappa_t\) must be supported on \(\ZZ\).
\end{observationprofilelemma}

\begin{proof}
The Fourier transform calculation in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling} gives
\[
\widehat F_t(\xi)
=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}\int_{\RR}s e^{-\ii s\xi}\,\kappa_t(\dd s).
\]
This is exactly \(q_{F_t}(\xi)=\pi\widehat{\lambda_t}(\xi)\).  Fourier
uniqueness for finite signed measures gives \(\lambda_t\).  On any Borel set
bounded away from the origin, multiplication by \(s^{-1}\) is bounded, so the
displayed inversion recovers \(\kappa_t\) there.  Increasing such sets to
\(\RR\setminus\{0\}\) recovers all off-origin mass, and the remaining mass is
the atom at \(0\) because \(\kappa_t\) is a probability measure.

The last assertion is the periodicity criterion proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling}: the integer-orbit
cardinal formula first recovers the projection \(\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\), and this
specific reconstruction formula represents the full channel precisely when
the law-side profile is \(2\pi\)-periodic.  Since \(\lambda_t=s\kappa_t\), the
periodicity condition is equivalent to
\((e^{-\ii2\pi s}-1)s\,\kappa_t(\dd s)=0\), hence to support of \(\kappa_t\) on
\(\ZZ\) after allowing the harmless atom at \(0\).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{theorem}[Integer-orbit projection of Poisson observation channels]%
\label{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling}
\label{prop:poisson-law-lattice-periodicity}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean
$\bar{\gamma}$, let $t>0$, and let $\mathfrak d_t$ be the centred
fluctuation profile from Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}.  Put
\[
  X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},
  \qquad
F_t:=\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t\in\mathcal H_K,
\]
and let $\Pi_{\ZZ}$ denote the orthogonal projection of $\mathcal H_K$ onto
$\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.  Define the integer-orbit observation samples
\[
\alpha_n(t):=F_t(n),\qquad n\in\ZZ .
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}, these samples are evaluations of
the centred Poisson observation profile against the secant modes at the
integer orbit.  The projection $\Pi_{\ZZ}$ is the lattice component of that
observation channel, with constants and norm formula determined by the exact
Poisson symbol \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}.  The reconstruction of the lattice
component uses the standard principal-symbol projection mechanism recorded in
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}.  Full ambient recovery is
equivalent to the law-side condition
\[
\nu\bigl(\{\gamma\in\RR:X_c\in t\ZZ\}\bigr)=1;
\]
the theorem lists the equivalent RKHS and periodicity formulations below.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-integer-samples}
\alpha_n(t)
=\langle \mathfrak d_t,\Psi_n\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=\frac1t\int_{\RR}
(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})\,
K\!\left(n,\frac{\gamma-\bar{\gamma}}{t}\right)\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad n\in\ZZ .
\end{equation}
Moreover $\alpha(t):=(\alpha_n(t))_{n\in\ZZ}\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-lattice-stability}
A_{\ZZ}\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|\alpha_n(t)|^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|\mathfrak d_t\|_{L^2(\omega)}^2.
\end{equation}
The cardinal reconstruction with these observable samples is the lattice
projection:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-projection}
\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t(z)
=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_n(t)L(z-n)
\qquad(z\in\mathbb S),
\end{equation}
with convergence in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and uniformly on compact subsets
of~$\mathbb S$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-norm}
\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha_t(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta,
\qquad
\widetilde\alpha_t(\theta):=
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_n(t)e^{-\ii n\theta}.
\end{equation}
Finally,
\[
F_t-\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)^\perp
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
(F_t-\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t)(n)=0\quad(n\in\ZZ).
\]
Thus the integer samples reconstruct $\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t$ from the data
$(F_t(n))_{n\in\ZZ}$.  The next display gives the law-side criterion for this
projection to equal the full observation channel.
Define the law-side spectral profile
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-profile}
Q_t(\xi)
:=\frac{\pi}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c
e^{-\ii X_c\xi/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad \xi\in\RR .
\end{equation}
Then $Q_t$ is bounded and continuous, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-q-profile}
q_{F_t}(\xi):=e^{2|\xi|}\widehat F_t(\xi)=Q_t(\xi)
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\xi\in\RR .
\end{equation}
Consequently, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the integer-orbit cardinal reconstruction in
      \eqref{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-projection} recovers the full
      Poisson observation channel $F_t$;
\item $F_t\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$;
\item the law-side profile $Q_t$ is $2\pi$-periodic, equivalently
      \begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-periodicity}
      \int_{\RR}X_c e^{-\ii X_c(\xi+2\pi)/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
      =
      \int_{\RR}X_c e^{-\ii X_c\xi/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
      \qquad(\xi\in\RR).
      \end{equation}
\item the centred law has no mass off the lattice $t\ZZ$, equivalently
      \begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-lattice-support}

[32m succeeded in 217ms:[0m
e^{-|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi)=0
\]
as tempered distributions.  Let
\(U\Subset(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)\).  On \(U\), the function
\(e^{-|\xi|}\) is \(C^\infty\) and nonvanishing, so multiplication by
\(e^{|\xi|}\) is legitimate on test functions supported in \(U\).  Hence
\(\widehat f=0\) on every such \(U\), and therefore
\[
\operatorname{supp}\widehat f\subset\{0\}.
\]
A tempered distribution supported at one point has the form
\[
\widehat f=\sum_{j=0}^{J}c_j\delta_0^{(j)} .
\]
Consequently \(f\) is a polynomial.  Since
\(f\in L^2(\omega)\), no polynomial of degree at least one can occur:
with \(\omega(\dd y)=\pi^{-1}(1+y^2)^{-1}\dd y\), the square of such a
polynomial is not \(\omega\)-integrable at infinity.  Thus \(f\) is
constant.  The zero-mean condition in \(L^2_0(\omega)\) forces that
constant to be \(0\), so \(f=0\) in \(L^2(\omega)\).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{theorem}[The mode space and its RKHS completion]%
\label{thm:mode-space-rkhs}
Let
\[
L^2_0(\omega):=
\left\{
f\in L^2(\omega):\ \int_{\RR}f(y)\,\omega(\dd y)=0
\right\},
\]
and let $\mathcal H_K$ be the reproducing-kernel Hilbert space generated
by the kernel
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2}.
\]
Set
\[
\mathcal S_0:=\operatorname{span}\{\Psi_\varepsilon:\varepsilon\in\RR\},
\qquad
\mathcal S:=\overline{\mathcal S_0}^{\,L^2(\omega)}
\subset L^2_0(\omega),
\]
Then
\[
\mathcal S=L^2_0(\omega).
\]
Equivalently, the algebraic span
$\mathcal S_0$ is dense in~$L^2_0(\omega)$.
The density assertion rests on the isolated Poisson-transform completeness
lemma immediately above: orthogonality to all secant kernels, together with
the zero-mean condition, says that the Poisson transform \(P_1*f\) vanishes
on the line.  Fourier injectivity of the strictly positive Poisson multiplier
then forces \(f=0\).
Moreover, the linear assignment
\[
\mathcal U_0:\mathcal S_0\longrightarrow \mathcal H_K,
\qquad
\mathcal U_0(\Psi_\varepsilon):=K(\cdot,\varepsilon)
\qquad(\varepsilon\in\RR)
\]
is an isometry and extends uniquely to a unitary operator
\[
\mathcal U:L^2_0(\omega)\longrightarrow \mathcal H_K.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We already noted that $\Psi_\varepsilon\in L^2_0(\omega)$, so
$\mathcal S_0\subset L^2_0(\omega)$.  Let
$f\in L^2_0(\omega)$ be orthogonal to~$\mathcal S_0$.  Then for every
$\varepsilon\in\RR$,
\[
0=\langle f,\Psi_\varepsilon\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}.
\]
  The supplementary completeness lemma cited in the statement applies and
  gives $f=0$.
Therefore $\mathcal S_0^\perp=\{0\}$ in $L^2_0(\omega)$, so
\[
\overline{\mathcal S_0}^{\,L^2(\omega)}=L^2_0(\omega).
\]
By definition this closure is~$\mathcal S$, hence
$\mathcal S=L^2_0(\omega)$.

Theorem~\ref{thm:mode-gram-kernel} shows that
\[
\langle \Psi_\varepsilon,\Psi_\eta\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=K(\varepsilon,\eta)
=\langle K(\cdot,\varepsilon),K(\cdot,\eta)\rangle_{\mathcal H_K},
\]
so $\mathcal U_0$ is an isometry on~$\mathcal S_0$.  Since the
kernel sections span a dense subspace of~$\mathcal H_K$, the isometry
extends uniquely to a surjective isometry
$\mathcal U:L^2_0(\omega)\to\mathcal H_K$, that is, to a unitary
operator.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Centred reconstruction from Poisson data}

\begin{proposition}[Centred Poisson reconstruction by Laplace uniqueness]%
\label{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean~$\bar{\gamma}$.
Let $\nu_c$ denote the law of the centred variable
$X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}$.  Define
\[
h_t(x):=\int_{\RR}P_t(x-\gamma)\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
the central observation functions
\[
A(t):=\pi\,h_t(\bar{\gamma}),
\qquad
B(t):=\pi\,\partial_x h_t(x)\big|_{x=\bar{\gamma}}
\qquad(t>0).
\]
\[
H(t):=\int_t^\infty B(s)\dd s
\qquad(t>0),
\]
and let
\[
\phi_{\nu_c}(r):=\int_{\RR}e^{irX_c}\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad r\in\RR,
\]
be the centred characteristic function.  Then for every $t>0$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-laplace-reconstruction}
A(t)+\ii H(t)
=\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\dd r.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the pair $(A,B)$ determines the centred law~$\nu_c$
uniquely.  Equivalently, the triple $(\bar{\gamma},A,B)$ determines the
original measure~$\nu$ uniquely.
\end{proposition}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_gram_space.tex}{the proof following Proposition
``Centred Poisson reconstruction by Laplace uniqueness''}
\else
\begin{proof}
By the elementary Laplace identities
\[
\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\cos(rX_c)\dd r=\frac{t}{t^2+X_c^2},
\qquad
\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\sin(rX_c)\dd r=\frac{X_c}{t^2+X_c^2},
\]
one obtains
\[
A(t)
=\int_{\RR}\frac{t}{t^2+X_c^2}\dd\nu(\gamma)
=\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\Re\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\dd r.
\]
Differentiation under the integral is justified by dominated convergence,
since for each fixed $t>0$,
\[
\left|\frac{2t(x-\gamma)}{((x-\gamma)^2+t^2)^2}\right|
\le \frac{9}{8\sqrt3}\,t^{-2}
\qquad(x,\gamma\in\RR),
\]
and
\[
\partial_x h_t(x)\big|_{x=\bar{\gamma}}
=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{\RR}\frac{t\,X_c}{(X_c^2+t^2)^2}\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
in particular
\[
B(t)=2t\int_{\RR}\frac{X_c}{(X_c^2+t^2)^2}\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
Since
\[
\int_t^\infty \frac{2s|X_c|}{(X_c^2+s^2)^2}\dd s
=\frac{|X_c|}{X_c^2+t^2}
\le \frac{1}{2t},
\]
Fubini's theorem applies and gives
\[
H(t)
=\int_{\RR}\frac{X_c}{t^2+X_c^2}\dd\nu(\gamma)
=\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\Im\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\dd r.
\]
Combining the two formulas yields
\eqref{eq:poisson-laplace-reconstruction}.  Let
\[
F(t):=A(t)+\ii H(t)
=\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\dd r,
\qquad t>0 .
\]
By uniqueness of the Laplace transform on bounded continuous functions
\cite{Widder1941}, \(F\) determines \(\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\) for every
\(r\ge0\).  Since \(\nu_c\) is a probability measure on \(\RR\), its
characteristic function satisfies the conjugate symmetry
\[
\phi_{\nu_c}(-r)=\overline{\phi_{\nu_c}(r)}
\qquad(r\ge0).
\]
Thus the values on \( [0,\infty) \) determine the values on all of
\(\RR\).  Levy's continuity/inversion theorem for characteristic functions
then recovers the centred law~$\nu_c$ \cite{Lukacs1970,Feller1971}, and
adding the mean recovers~$\nu$.
\end{proof}
\fi

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The two symmetry corollaries and their proofs are in
\path{sec_gram_space.tex}, subsection ``Centred reconstruction from Poisson
data,'' collected by \path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
\begin{corollary}[Symmetric channel]%
\label{cor:poisson-symmetrization}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}, the
function $A$ determines the symmetrization of the centred law~$\nu_c$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The real part of $\phi_{\nu_c}$ is exactly the characteristic function of
the centred symmetrization
$\frac12(\nu_c+\check\nu_c)$, where $\check\nu_c(E):=\nu_c(-E)$.  Since
$A$ is the Laplace transform of $\Re\phi_{\nu_c}$ on $[0,\infty)$, Laplace
inversion recovers that real part on the positive half-line.  Conjugate
symmetry makes $\Re\phi_{\nu_c}$ even, so this determines the whole
characteristic function of the symmetrized law.  This is the even channel.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Symmetric case from the odd channel]%
\label{cor:poisson-symmetric-case}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}, the
centred law~$\nu_c$ is symmetric if and only if $B\equiv 0$.  In that case
$A$ alone determines~$\nu_c$, and the pair $(\bar{\gamma},A)$ determines the
original law~$\nu$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The centred law is symmetric if and only if
$\Im\phi_{\nu_c}\equiv 0$ on~$\RR$.  By
\eqref{eq:poisson-laplace-reconstruction} and Laplace uniqueness,
\(B\equiv0\) is equivalent to \(H\equiv0\), hence to
\(\Im\phi_{\nu_c}\equiv0\) on \([0,\infty)\).  Conjugate symmetry then makes
\(\Im\phi_{\nu_c}\equiv0\) on all of \(\RR\), which is equivalent to
symmetry of~\(\nu_c\).  This is the odd channel obstruction: when it
vanishes, the centred law agrees with its symmetrization.  Therefore
Corollary~\ref{cor:poisson-symmetrization} shows that $A$ determines~$\nu_c$.
Translating by the known mean $\bar{\gamma}$ then determines~$\nu$.
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{theorem}[Observation channels as evaluation functionals]%
\label{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean~$\bar{\gamma}$.
For $t>0$ define the centred fluctuation profile
\[
\mathfrak d_t(y):=
\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}{g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}-1,
\qquad y\in\RR.
\]
Then $\mathfrak d_t\in L^2_0(\omega)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-secant}
\mathfrak d_t(y)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\,\Psi_{X_c/t}(y)\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-rkhs}
(\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t)(a)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\,K(a,X_c/t)\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad a\in\RR.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-origin}
\mathfrak d_t(0)=tA(t)-1,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-rkhs-origin}
(\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t)(0)=2t\,H(2t)
=2t\int_{2t}^{\infty}B(s)\,\dd s.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By definition,
\[
\mathfrak d_t(y)
=\int_{\RR}\bigl(R_{X_c/t}(y)-1\bigr)\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\,\Psi_{X_c/t}(y)\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
which is \eqref{eq:delta-t-secant}.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:mode-gram-kernel},
\[
\|\Psi_\varepsilon\|_{L^2(\omega)}^2
=\frac{2}{4+(\varepsilon-\varepsilon)^2}
=\frac12.
\]
Hence
\[
\left\|\mathfrak d_t\right\|_{L^2(\omega)}
\le\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}|X_c|\,\left\|\Psi_{X_c/t}\right\|_{L^2(\omega)}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
=\frac{1}{\sqrt2\,t}\int_{\RR}|X_c|\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
<\infty,
\]
so \eqref{eq:delta-t-secant} is a Bochner integral in $L^2_0(\omega)$.
Therefore,
\[
\int_{\RR}\mathfrak d_t(y)\,\omega(\dd y)
=\int_{\RR}(h_t(x)-g_t(x))\,\dd x=0,
\]
and thus $\mathfrak d_t\in L^2_0(\omega)$.  Applying the unitary map
$\mathcal U$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:mode-space-rkhs} to
\eqref{eq:delta-t-secant} yields \eqref{eq:delta-t-rkhs}.

At $y=0$ one has
\[
g_t(\bar{\gamma})=\frac{1}{\pi t},
\]
so
\[
\mathfrak d_t(0)
=\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma})}{g_t(\bar{\gamma})}-1
=tA(t)-1,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:delta-t-origin}.  Finally, evaluating
\eqref{eq:delta-t-rkhs} at $a=0$ and using
$K(0,b)=2/(4+b^2)$ gives
\[
(\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t)(0)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\frac{2}{4+(X_c/t)^2}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
=2t\int_{\RR}\frac{X_c}{4t^2+X_c^2}\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
The definition of $H$ in
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel} gives
\[
H(2t)=\int_{\RR}\frac{X_c}{4t^2+X_c^2}\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
and \eqref{eq:delta-t-rkhs-origin} follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The previous theorem recovers the centred Cauchy transform on the
imaginary axis:
\[
G_{\nu_c}(\ii t)
=\int_{\RR}\frac{1}{\ii t-X_c}\dd\nu(\gamma)
=-H(t)-\ii A(t)
\qquad(t>0).
\]
Thus the Laplace-transform formulation strengthens the earlier
Cauchy-transform identity while also isolating the loss of the translation
parameter.  In particular, the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel} is better viewed as a
Laplace-uniqueness plus characteristic-function-inversion argument than
as a Stieltjes inversion argument.
\end{remark}

% sec_strip_lattice.tex -- Strip RKHS and lattice consequences
\section{The Strip RKHS and Lattice Consequences}%
\label{sec:strip-lattice}

\paragraph{Complex Hilbert space convention.}
From this point onward, we work with the complexification of the real
Hilbert space from Appendix~\ref{sec:gram-space}, still denoted
$\mathcal H_K$.  Its inner product is linear in the first variable and
conjugate-linear in the second:
\[
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal H_K}
:=\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi)\,
\overline{\widehat g(\xi)}\,\dd\xi.
\]
All reproducing kernels satisfy the Hermitian symmetry
$K(z,w)=\overline{K(w,z)}$, and evaluation representers depend on
$\overline w$, not on~$w$, in the second slot.

\paragraph{Role of the strip and lattice results.}
This appendix records the integer-orbit projection theorem for Poisson
observation channels.  The standard strip, Hardy, and principal
shift-invariant ingredients are included to support that projection statement
and to make clear where standard sampling theory enters.
Proposition~\ref{prop:gram-space-poisson-image}
identifies $\mathcal H_K$ with the Poisson image space
$\sqrt{\pi}\,e^{-|D|}L^2(\RR)$ and provides the strip model.  The subsequent
Hardy splitting is included as classical model identification: it names the
positive and negative Fourier-support summands of this strip RKHS and fixes
their kernels.  For the lattice part we use the standard principal
shift-invariant-space mechanism on the closed integer-orbit spaces
$\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and $\mathcal S_{\ZZ}$.
The background part is the positive-symbol/Riesz-basis/cardinal machinery.  The
integer-orbit chain is
the integer-projection lemma in \path{sec_strip_10_hardy_aux_lattice.tex},
subsection ``Hardy splitting and the integer-orbit projection,''
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}, the explicit-cardinal-kernel
corollary in \path{sec_strip_30_cardinal_observation.tex}, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling}.  The
Poisson-specific part is the closed hyperbolic symbol
\eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}, the Fourier and reciprocal-symbol norm formulas in
that cardinal-kernel corollary, and the final projection dichotomy for the
actual observation profiles from Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}:
integer samples recover exactly the lattice projection of the observation
channel, and recover the full channel precisely when the centred law is
supported on \(t\ZZ\).  The separate inversion step from the full observation
profile to the scaled centred law is recorded in
\path{sec_strip_30_cardinal_observation.tex}, Lemma ``Observation profile
determines the centred law.''  No result below uses a
variable lattice spacing; the only lattice case retained here is the integer
orbit.

\subsection{A Poisson image realization of the mode space}

\begin{proposition}[The mode Gram space as a Poisson image space]%
\label{prop:gram-space-poisson-image}
Let
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2}=\pi\,P_2(a-b),
\qquad a,b\in\RR,
\]
and let $\mathcal H_K$ be the reproducing-kernel Hilbert space generated
by~$K$.  Define
\[
\mathcal X
:=
\left\{
f\in L^2(\RR):
\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\abs{\widehat f(\xi)}^2\dd\xi<\infty
\right\},
\]
with norm
\[
\|f\|_{\mathcal X}^2
:=\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\abs{\widehat f(\xi)}^2\dd\xi.
\]
Then $\mathcal H_K=\mathcal X$ isometrically.  Equivalently,
\[
\mathcal H_K=\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1(L^2(\RR)),
\qquad
\|\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1 g\|_{\mathcal H_K}=\|g\|_{L^2(\RR)}
\quad(g\in L^2(\RR)).
\]
Every $f\in\mathcal H_K$ admits a unique holomorphic extension to the
strip
\[
\mathbb S:=\{z\in\CC:\ |\Im z|<1\},
\]
given by
\[
f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii z\xi}\dd\xi,
\qquad z\in\mathbb S,
\]
and satisfying the sharp pointwise estimate
\[
|f(x+\ii y)|\le \frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1-y^2)}}\,\|f\|_{\mathcal H_K}
\qquad(x\in\RR,\ |y|<1).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Fourier transform of the translation kernel $k(x):=2/(4+x^2)$ is
\[
\widehat k(\xi)=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}.
\]
Hence
\[
\widehat{K(\cdot,a)}(\xi)=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}e^{-\ii a\xi}.
\]
For $f\in\mathcal X$ one therefore has
\begin{align*}
\langle f,K(\cdot,a)\rangle_{\mathcal X}
&=
\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{\RR}
e^{2|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi)\,
\overline{\widehat{K(\cdot,a)}(\xi)}\dd\xi \\
&=
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii a\xi}\dd\xi
=f(a)
\end{align*}
by Fourier inversion.  Thus $\mathcal X$ is a reproducing-kernel Hilbert
space with kernel~$K$, and the Moore--Aronszajn uniqueness theorem yields
$\mathcal X=\mathcal H_K$.

Now let $g\in L^2(\RR)$ and set $f:=\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1 g$.  Since
\[
\widehat f(\xi)=\sqrt{\pi}\,e^{-|\xi|}\widehat g(\xi),
\]
Plancherel gives
\[
\|f\|_{\mathcal X}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\pi e^{-2|\xi|}
\abs{\widehat g(\xi)}^2\dd\xi
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\abs{\widehat g(\xi)}^2\dd\xi
=\|g\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
Conversely, if $f\in\mathcal X$, define
\[
\widehat g(\xi):=\pi^{-1/2}e^{|\xi|}\widehat f(\xi).
\]
Then $g\in L^2(\RR)$ and
$\widehat f=\sqrt{\pi}\,e^{-|\xi|}\widehat g$, so
$f=\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1 g$.  This proves the image-space description.

If $z=x+\ii y$ with $|y|<1$, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{align*}
|f(z)|
&\le \frac{1}{2\pi}
\left(\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\abs{\widehat f(\xi)}^2\dd\xi\right)^{1/2}
\left(\int_{\RR}e^{-2|\xi|}e^{-2y\xi}\dd\xi\right)^{1/2} \\
&=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1-y^2)}}\,\|f\|_{\mathcal X},
\end{align*}
because
\[
\int_{\RR}e^{-2|\xi|}e^{-2y\xi}\dd\xi=\frac{1}{1-y^2}.
\]
The constant is sharp: at each $z=x+\ii y$ equality is attained by the
normalized reproducing kernel whose Fourier transform is proportional to
$e^{-2|\xi|}e^{-\ii\overline z\xi}$.
Hence the integral defining $f(z)$ converges absolutely and locally
uniformly on $\mathbb S$, so the extension is holomorphic there.  The same
estimate shows uniqueness by continuity from the real axis.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:poisson-image-framework}
The identity $K(a,b) = \pi P_2(a-b)$ means that the Gram kernel is,
up to the factor~$\pi$, the Poisson kernel at time~$2$.  This explains
why the RKHS is a Poisson image space: the kernel itself arises from the
semigroup.  The holomorphic extension and pointwise estimate above are
the standard $H^2$ Poisson-boundary statements transported to a
translation-invariant strip model.
\end{remark}

% sec_preliminaries.tex -- Preliminaries
\section{Cayley--Haar preliminaries}%
\label{sec:preliminaries}

We fix the notation and normalizations used throughout the paper.  The
same section also records the only circle-theoretic input needed later:
the Cayley chart identifies normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ with the
Cauchy weight on~$\RR$.

Write
\[
\TT:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}=1\},\qquad
\DD:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}<1\},\qquad
\UU:=\Bigl(-\tfrac12,\tfrac12\Bigr).
\]
The normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ is
\[
\dd m_{\TT}(e^{\ii\theta})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\dd\theta,
\qquad
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi].
\]
We write
\[
\overline\RR:=\RR\cup\{\infty\}
\]
for the one-point compactification of~$\RR$.  For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)$ we
use the Fourier transform
\[
\widehat f(\xi):=\int_{\RR}f(x)e^{-\ii x\xi}\dd x,
\qquad \xi\in\RR,
\]
so that $\widehat{f*g}=\widehat f\,\widehat g$ whenever both sides are
defined.
For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)\cap L^{2}(\RR)$ we also use the inversion and
Plancherel formulas
\[
f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii x\xi}\dd\xi,
\qquad
\|f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\|\widehat f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}
\]
\cite[Chapter~I]{Stein1971}.

If $X$ is a real-valued random variable with density $f_X$ with respect
to Lebesgue measure, its differential entropy is
\[
h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X):=-\int_{\RR}f_X(x)\log f_X(x)\dd x,
\]
whenever the integral is well defined.  If $f$ and $g$ are probability
density functions on~$\RR$, the Kullback--Leibler divergence
\cite{KullbackLeibler1951,CoverThomas2006} is used throughout as an
extended nonnegative functional.  Precisely, if \(f\not\ll g\), then
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g):=+\infty .
\]
If \(f\ll g\), write \(u=f/g\) on \(\{g>0\}\) and set \(u=1\) on
\(\{g=0\}\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extended-kl-definition}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)
:=\int_{\RR}\{u(x)\log u(x)-u(x)+1\}\,g(x)\dd x
\in[0,\infty],
\end{equation}
with the convention \(0\log0=0\).  Equivalently, when
\(f\log(f/g)\) is integrable in the ordinary sense, this equals
\[
\int_{\RR}f(x)\log\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\dd x .
\]
Thus the symbol \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}\) never presupposes finiteness.  In the
applications below \(g\) is a positive Poisson density, so absolute
continuity is automatic; under finite centred second moment
Lemma~\ref{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient} gives
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))<\infty\) for every
\(t>0\).  Without that finite-variance hypothesis, all KL statements are
read in the extended interval \([0,\infty]\).

\begin{lemma}[KL bounded-test and Poisson quotient estimates]%
\label{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient}
\label{lem:bounded-test-pinsker}
\label{lem:finite-time-poisson-kl-finite}
\label{lem:sharp-threshold-bounded-test-pinsker}
The following elementary estimates will be used repeatedly.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(f\) and \(g\) be probability densities on~\(\RR\), and interpret
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)\) by \eqref{eq:extended-kl-definition}.  If
\(\varphi\in L^\infty(\RR)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(x)\{f(x)-g(x)\}\dd x\right|^2
\le 2\|\varphi\|_\infty^2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\end{equation}
The inequality is an extended-valued statement: if the right-hand side is
\(+\infty\), it is read as a trivial bound.

\item Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean
\(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
For every compact interval \(0<a<b<\infty\), the quotient \(u_t\) is bounded
above and below by positive constants uniformly for
\((t,x)\in[a,b]\times\RR\).  More precisely, with
\(\mu_2=\int(\gamma-\bar\gamma)^2\dd\nu(\gamma)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-poisson-quotient-two-sided-bound}
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad(t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR).
\end{equation}

\item Under the hypotheses of the preceding item,
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)<\infty\qquad(t>0).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the bounded-test estimate, if \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)=+\infty\) there is
nothing to prove.  Otherwise \(f\ll g\), and the usual
Csisz{\'a}r--Pinsker inequality for natural logarithms gives
\[
\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)}^2\le 2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\]
Since \(\varphi\) is bounded,
\[
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(f-g)\dd x\right|
\le \|\varphi\|_\infty\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)} ,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}.

Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and
finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
Write \(X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\), \(z=(x-\bar{\gamma})/t\), and
\[
R_\varepsilon(z):=\frac{1+z^2}{1+(z-\varepsilon)^2}.
\]
Then
\[
u_t(x)=\int_{\RR}R_{X_c/t}(z)\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
The elementary inequalities
\[
R_\varepsilon(z)\le 2(1+\varepsilon^2),
\qquad
R_\varepsilon(z)\ge \frac{1}{2(1+\varepsilon^2)}
\]
give, with \(\mu_2=\EE X_c^2\),
\[
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad (t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR),
\]
where the lower bound uses Jensen's inequality for
\(s\mapsto(1+s)^{-1}\).  Fix \(t>0\) and choose \(a<t<b\).  If
\(m\le u_t\le M\), then
\[
0\le D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR}u_t(x)\log u_t(x)\,g_t(x)\dd x
\le M\max\{|\log m|,|\log M|\}<\infty .
\]
\end{proof}
We shall repeatedly use the elementary identity
\[
\int_{\RR}(f-g)\dd x=0
\]
whenever $f$ and $g$ are probability densities.

% sec_cayley_gate.tex -- The Cayley transform
\subsection{The Cayley chart}\label{sec:cayley-gate}

We work with the Cayley coordinates
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phase-coord}
\upsilon(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan x\in \UU,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:angular-var}
\vartheta(x):=2\pi\upsilon(x)=2\arctan x\in(-\pi,\pi),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-embed}
\iota(x):=e^{\ii\vartheta(x)}=\frac{1+\ii x}{1-\ii x}\in\TT,
\qquad x\in\RR.
\end{equation}
Thus $\upsilon$ identifies $\RR$ with the interval $\UU$, while $\iota$
identifies $\RR$ with $\TT\setminus\{-1\}$.  We shall use these
coordinates repeatedly.

\begin{proposition}[Cayley compactification]\label{prop:cayley-homeo}
The map $\iota$ extends to a homeomorphism
\[
\iota:\overline\RR\xrightarrow{\sim}\TT
\]
by $\iota(\infty)=-1$.  Its inverse is given by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-inverse}
\iota^{-1}(e^{\ii\theta})=\tan\frac{\theta}{2},
\qquad
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi),
\qquad
\iota^{-1}(-1)=\infty.
\end{equation}
Moreover, $\upsilon:\RR\to\UU$ and
$\iota:\RR\to\TT\setminus\{-1\}$ are $C^\infty$ diffeomorphisms.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The identity \eqref{eq:cayley-embed} shows that $\iota$ is a smooth
bijection from $\RR$ onto $\TT\setminus\{-1\}$ with inverse
$x=\tan(\theta/2)$.  Since $\iota(x)\to -1$ as $x\to\pm\infty$, the map
extends continuously to~$\overline\RR$ by $\iota(\infty)=-1$.
Compactness of~$\overline\RR$ and the Hausdorff property of~$\TT$
imply that the extension is a homeomorphism.  The formulas
$x=\tan(\pi u)$ and \eqref{eq:cayley-inverse} give the smooth inverses
of $\upsilon$ and~$\iota$ on the open parts.
\end{proof}

% sec_haar_pullback.tex -- The Haar pullback
\subsection{Pullback of Haar measure}%
\label{sec:haar-pullback}

The Jacobian of the Cayley parametrization identifies normalized arc
length on the circle with the Cauchy weight on the line.  Since this is
the only role played by the circle model in the sequel, Proposition~\ref{prop:phase-derivative}
records just the change-of-variables formula used in the coefficient
computations.  The same Cauchy/wrapped-Cauchy M{\"o}bius normalization is a
standard one in circular statistics
\cite{KentTyler1988WrappedCauchy,McCullagh1992CauchyModels,
McCullagh1996CauchyMobius,
KatoJones2010MobiusCircle,KatoMcCullagh2020SphereCauchyMobius}.

Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cauchy-measure}
\omega(\dd x):=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^{2})}\dd x.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Cauchy phase Jacobian]\label{prop:phase-derivative}
The phase coordinate \eqref{eq:phase-coord} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phase-deriv}
\upsilon'(x)=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^{2})},
\qquad
\vartheta'(x)=\frac{2}{1+x^{2}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, $\upsilon:(\RR,\omega)\to(\UU,\dd u)$ is measure preserving.
In particular, for every non-negative Borel function $f$ on~$\UU$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-integral}
\int_{\RR}f(\upsilon(x))\,\omega(\dd x)=\int_{\UU}f(u)\dd u.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate \eqref{eq:phase-coord}.  The change-of-variables formula
then gives \eqref{eq:weighted-integral}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Endpoint-normalized chart]\label{rem:haar-pullback-normalization}
For the Cayley map itself the measure statement can be written in the two
equivalent forms
\begin{equation}\label{eq:haar-pullback}
\iota_{\#}\omega=m_{\TT},
\qquad
\iota^{*}m_{\TT}:=(\iota^{-1})_{\#}m_{\TT}=\omega.
\end{equation}
Here \(\iota_{\#}\) denotes pushforward from the line compactification to
the circle, while the displayed pullback notation is used only in this
homeomorphic chart sense.
This identity is the normalization used later for every Cauchy-weighted
mode integral and Laurent constant-term extraction.  It also fixes the
endpoint-normalized chart: if an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
\(\varphi:\overline\RR\to\TT\) has \(C^1\) restriction to \(\RR\),
\(\varphi(0)=1\), \(\varphi(\infty)=-1\), and
\(\varphi_{\#}\omega=m_{\TT}\), then writing
\(\varphi(x)=e^{\ii\alpha(x)}\) with \(\alpha(0)=0\) gives
\((2\pi)^{-1}\alpha'(x)\dd x=\omega(\dd x)\), hence
\(\alpha(x)=2\arctan x\) and \(\varphi=\iota\).  Reversing orientation only
adds the conjugate chart.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Notation guide for entropy coefficients}
\label{subsec:notation-guide-entropy}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The coefficient-notation table and the accompanying normalization references
are in \path{sec_haar_pullback.tex}, subsection ``Notation guide for entropy
coefficients,'' collected by \path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
The entropy arguments use several symbols that look similar but live in
different layers of the proof.  The following table fixes the convention used
after the Cayley--Haar preliminaries.  In particular, \(Q_n\) is always a
one-dimensional Laurent mode, while \(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\) is a covariance
quadratic form; the subscript \(d\) in the latter denotes dimension, not a
Laurent index.
The analytic language used later is standard: the Poisson--Cauchy semigroup
and its generator are those of the symmetric \(1\)-stable process
\cite{Feller1971,Bertoin1996LevyProcesses,Sato1999LevyProcesses,
Applebaum2009LevyProcesses,Kwasnicki2017}, and the entropy/Bregman notation is
the Kullback--Leibler specialization of the Csisz{\'a}r--Morimoto--Ali--Silvey
\(f\)-divergence calculus
\cite{KullbackLeibler1951,AliSilvey1966Divergence,Csiszar1967,
Morimoto1963Markov,Vajda1989TheoryDivergence,CoverThomas2006,Bregman1967}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{0.28\textwidth}p{0.22\textwidth}X@{}}
\textbf{Role} & \textbf{Notation} & \textbf{Meaning and scope} \\
\hline
One-dimensional coefficient &
\(A_k^{\rm form}\), \(A_k(\nu)\) &
\(A_k^{\rm form}\) is the universal polynomial obtained from the
Cayley--Chebyshev mode integrals.  \(A_k(\nu)\) is its evaluation at the
centred moments of the one-dimensional input law, when those moments exist. \\
Multidimensional coefficient &
\(\mathcal A_m^{(d)}(\nu)\) &
The retained coefficient in the \(d\)-dimensional radial Poisson KL expansion.
We use the calligraphic symbol throughout and do not introduce a separate
\(A_m^{(d)}\) alias. \\
Laurent mode &
\(Q_n(z)\) &
The Laurent representative of the one-dimensional mode \(u_n\), defined by
\(Q_n(e^{2\ii\theta})=u_n(\tan\theta)\).  Its index \(n\) is a mode order. \\
Covariance quadratic form &
\(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\) &
The algebraic quadratic contraction determined by a \(d\)-dimensional
covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  It becomes a KL coefficient only after a
sufficient quotient jet has supplied the leading \(t^{-2}\) density layer. \\
Dissipation &
\(\mathcal I_\nu(t)\) &
The nonlocal Bregman dissipation of the moving matched Cauchy quotient,
defined by annular cutoffs and used in the tail identity
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)=\int_t^\infty\mathcal I_\nu(s)\dd s\). \\
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
\fi
